## Primary Request and Intent
- Train a good anime tagger using **DINOv3-ViT/B/16** as frozen base + **OFTv2** (Orthogonal Fine-Tune, "a type of lora") adapter, optimizer **SinkSGD_ADV** ("which is muon-like").
- Verbatim constraints: "I don't want to wait around too much, so I want to train only a maximum of 10,000 pictures in one epoch." "I want maximum training efficiency. That means BF16 is used where appropriate." "Ask me questions if needed!"
- Dataset sizes: 10,000 training pictures, 1,000 validation pictures, 1,000 test pictures.
- **Goal**: "The goal is to beat convnextv2_huge... Beat it in our test of 1,000 test images. Construct and evaluate convnextv2_huge against Macro(F1/MCC/P/R), Micro(F1/MCC/P/R) on our test of 1,000 images. Then that will be our bar we have to beat. We probably won't beat it using only 10,000 training images, but it's worth a shot."
- Caching spec: rescale to 512x512 bilinear, save as 0.5GB `.pt` chunks in `I:/.cache/wd-tagger-trainer/{train|test|valid}/`, name `shard#####-{total images}.pt`, pausable/resumable, cache is temporary → check at run start, build if missing.
- Training spec: pausable/resumable via `.PUASE`/`.PAUSE` file at folder root; output every 200 steps to `$WS/runs/{sha256 8-char truncation of config}/{some name}.pt`; **"When resuming, take care to not show the same picture you would have for step 1, for the resumed steps"**.
- Evaluation spec: at the very end, using test cache, to `$WS/runs/{sha8}/{someFile}`.
- Debug spec: "output the debug of the pictures as how the trainer saw it (as individual .png), for the first 100 pictures as `$WS/runs/{sha256 8 character truncation of config}/debugpics/({pictureName}-{picture-TarOrigination})`".
- User answers to clarifying questions: multiple epochs with best-on-val (not 1 epoch); **both resize arms** ("I believe option 3 is better. can we try options 1 and 3 as arms?") → arm "pad" (pad to white square then bilinear resize, matches baseline native preproc) and arm "squash" (plain bilinear resize to 512x512); thresholds: "0.5 primary + per-tag tuned secondary".
- Most recent request (unanswered): "any way to make this more efficient? I was getting like 9 million pixel

- Train a good anime tagger using **DINOv3-ViT/B/16** as frozen base + **OFTv2** (Orthogonal Fine-Tune, "a type of lora") adapter, optimizer **SinkSGD_ADV** ("which is muon-like").

- Dataset sizes: 10,000 training pictures, 1,000 validation pictures, 1,000 test pictures.
- **Goal**: "The goal is to beat convnextv2_huge... Beat it in our test of 1,000 test images. Construct and evaluate convnextv2_huge against Macro(F1/MCC/P/R), Micro(F1/MCC/P/R) on our test of 1,000 images. Then that will be our bar we have to beat. We probably won't beat it using only 10,000 training images, but it's worth a shot."

## Primary Request and Intent
- Train a good anime tagger using **DINOv3-ViT/B/16** as frozen base + **OFTv2** (Orthogonal Fine-Tune, "a type of lora") adapter, optimizer **SinkSGD_ADV** ("which is muon-like").
- Verbatim constraints: "I don't want to wait around too much, so I want to train only a maximum of 10,000 pictures in one epoch." "I want maximum training efficiency. That means BF16 is used where appropriate." "Ask me questions if needed!"
- Dataset sizes: 10,000 training pictures, 1,000 validation pictures, 1,000 test pictures.
- **Goal**: "The goal is to beat convnextv2_huge... Beat it in our test of 1,000 test images. Construct and evaluate convnextv2_huge against Macro(F1/MCC/P/R), Micro(F1/MCC/P/R) on our test of 1,000 images. Then that will be our bar we have to beat. We probably won't beat it using only 10,000 training images, but it's worth a shot."
- Caching spec: rescale to 512x512 bilinear, save as 0.5GB `.pt` chunks in `I:/.cache/wd-tagger-trainer/{train|test|valid}/`, name `shard#####-{total images}.pt`, pausable/resumable, cache is temporary → check at run start, build if missing.
- Training spec: pausable/resumable via `.PUASE`/`.PAUSE` file at folder root; output every 200 steps to `$WS/runs/{sha256 8-char truncation of config}/{some name}.pt`; **"When resuming, take care to not show the same picture you would have for step 1, for the resumed steps"**.
- Evaluation spec: at the very end, using test cache, to `$WS/runs/{sha8}/{someFile}`.
- Debug spec: "output the debug of the pictures as how the trainer saw it (as individual .png), for the first 100 pictures as `$WS/runs/{sha256 8 character truncation of config}/debugpics/({pictureName}-{picture-TarOrigination})`".
- User answers to clarifying questions: multiple epochs with best-on-val (not 1 epoch); **both resize arms** ("I believe option 3 is better. can we try options 1 and 3 as arms?") → arm "pad" (pad to white square then bilinear resize, matches baseline native preproc) and arm "squash" (plain bilinear resize to 512x512); thresholds: "0.5 primary + per-tag tuned secondary".

Fetched the exact numbers from the local copy, and the config settles three things at once:

| | bar `convnextv2_huge.dbv4-full` | us `4c78a58e` |
|---|---|---|
| **total params** | **692.6M** (exact, from safetensors header) | 107.5M |
| feature extractor | **657.5M**, fully fine-tuned | 87.0M, **frozen** |
| classifier head | **35.1M** (2,816 → 12,476) | **19.18M** (1,536 → 12,476) |
| adapter | — | 1.30M |
| **trainable** | **692.6M** | **20.48M** |
| input | 512×512, ImageNet mean/std | 512×512, ImageNet mean/std ✓ |

So it's not ~600M — it's **692.6M**, and we're training **3.0% of it**, with a backbone **7.6× smaller**.

**Three findings I didn't expect:**

1. **`num_classes: 12476`** — numerically identical to ours. That's not luck: `load_label_space()` builds our vocabulary from this model's own tag list. The comparison is apples-to-apples in label space by construction, which is a nice validation of the whole setup.

2. **`input_size: [3, 512, 512]` with ImageNet mean/std** — exactly our protocol. Both models are in-distribution at 512. Good.

3. **Their head is nearly 2× ours**: 35.1M parameters reading a 2,816-dim feature, versus our 19.18M reading a 1,536-dim feature. We're behind on classifier capacity too, not just backbone capacity — and that one is trivially fixable.

That last point deserves emphasis because it's the cheapest lever on the board that I hadn't listed. Our 1,536 dims is `cls_mean` — 768 from the CLS token plus 768 from the patch mean. Their 2,816 is just ConvNeXt V2-Huge's final stage width. We could widen our pooled feature to ~3,072 (e.g. concat CLS + patch-mean + patch-max, or pool the last two blocks) and give the head **35M parameters for essentially zero compute cost** — the head is 0.3% of the step time. That's a strictly better use of parameters than anything else we've discussed per hour spent.

**And one thing the "ws" in the dataset name now explains.** `danbooru-wdtagger-v4-w640-ws-full` — I'd guess *ws* = white space. That matches exactly what we measured blind: the bar scores **0.2017 / 0.6935** on letterboxed-white-square input versus **0.1938 / 0.6800** on squash. It was trained on padded images, and our geometry ex

Everything's armed. Baseline is at ~step 8,200, so val@10,000 lands in ~10 minutes and the orchestrator will auto-pause then launch. Answering your two questions properly first.

## What "widening the head" means

Our head is one layer: `LayerNorm(pool_dim) → Linear(pool_dim → 12,476)`. "Width" here means **the length of the descriptor going into it**, not a hidden layer:

```
current:  cls(768) ⊕ patch_mean(768)                                  = 1,536 dims → 19.18M params
widened:  cls(768) ⊕ patch_mean(768) ⊕ patch_max(768) ⊕ patch_std(768) = 3,072 dims → 38.34M params
```

Same single linear layer, same 12,476 outputs. We're just handing it a richer description of each image, computed from the same frozen 1,029 patch tokens. Max pooling answers "is this concept present *anywhere* in the image" (which is what a tag decision is), and std answers "how much does it vary spatially".

I implemented it as a composable `head_pool` string rather than a hardcoded variant, so `"cls"`, `"cls_mean"`, `"cls_mean_max_std"` all work now, and I verified the old path still reports **exactly 20.48M trainable** — no regression to the running baseline.

## No — wider is not better, and I over-sold it last message

Two corrections to myself:

**1. Their 2,816 is incidental, not tuned.** I said "their head is nearly 2× ours" as though that were a capacity gap we should close. But 2,816 is just ConvNeXt V2-Huge's stage-4 channel count — a consequence of their architecture, not a hyperparameter anyone optimised for F1. Comparing 2,816 vs 1,536 tells us nothing about which is *better*.

**2. Width adds parameters, not information.** Extra descriptor dims that are correlated with the ones already there don't buy accuracy — they buy overfitting. And this dataset is exactly where that bites: the tail is 7,908 labels with almost no examples. My honest expectation is that a wider head **helps micro and may hurt macro**, because the extra capacity gets spent memorising rare tags instead of generalising them.

What would make it genuinely better is a real information gain (max encodes presence differently from mean), not merely more numbers. So: cheap to test, genuinely uncertain — which is

Wide-head verdict just landed — and it's a genuine (if small) win:

| at step 5,000 | control (`cls_mean`, 1,536d) | wide (`cls_mean_max_std`, 3,072d) | delta |
|---|---|---|---|
| micro F1 | 0.4673 | **0.4699** | +0.0026 |
| macro F1 | 0.0427 | **0.0480** | +0.0053 |
| supported macro | 0.1154 | **0.1297** | **+0.0143 (+12.4%)** |

Same seed, same images, same order, same LR — so the wide head is ahead on all three, and the biggest relative gain is on the tail, which is exactly where we need it. It's a small effect (within the noise band I warned about for micro), but it's not a loss, and it costs ~2% step time.

**And it leaves the overnight decision easy**: the wide run was launched with the *full* 110,870-step schedule and just stopped early via `--max-steps`. So I can **resume `b2a924f1` from step 5,020 to completion** — keeping the head that won, keeping its 5,020 steps, nothing discarded. That's the arm with the best chance of succeeding.

On your gradient_checkpointing question: **no, it's off** — `model.py:66` sets `self.backbone.gradient_checkpointing = False`. And you're right that it helps, for a non-obvious reason: with a frozen backbone you'd think activations are droppable, but our OFT adapters are *distributed through all layers*, so every block's input activation must be retained to compute OFT gradients. Checkpointing trades ~33% recompute for a large activation saving — exactly the lever for maximising L's batch size. Testing it now, since you're staying up for this:

- Latest turn (before sleep): asked me to work **autonomously overnight**, expects "the thing to be still training (if validation is still rising)". Their answers:
  - **overnight**: "you train the arm that has the best chance of succeeding :sunglasses:" (deliberately left to my judgement).
  - **plateau**: "keep going for another 2 validation numbers. if validation is worse monotonically for 3 nums (and also worse on maybe the full dataset validation *** done smartly), then you try a different idea (wider head? vit-L? other ideas maybe you can think of)". Also: "maybe we should increase our spot testing of valid to 5k or 10k pics. 1k is actually pretty snappy fast."
  - **probe**: "only vitB and vitL. no vitH+." For ViT-L, "try to maximize vram usage — I'll be present for this and stay up just a bit longer to monitor this specifically, so you can find optimal batch size for the ViT-L". ## Primary Request and Intent
- Train an anime tagger: frozen **DINOv3-ViT/B/16** + **OFTv2** adapter + **SinkSGD_ADV** optimizer ("which is muon-like"). Beat the bar `convnextv2_huge.dbv4-full` on a 1,000-image test across Macro(F1/MCC/P/R) and Micro(F1/MCC/P/R).
- Constraints: "I don't want to wait around too much, so I want to train only a maximum of 10,000 pictures in one epoch." "I want maximum training efficiency. That means BF16 is used where appropriate." "Ask me questions if needed!"
- Escalated to the 5.3M-image full-corpus run: **lr_head 0.00012 unchanged**, **1 pass**, **batch 48**.
- Overnight autonomy: "you train the arm that has the best chance of succeeding :sunglasses:"; plateau rule "keep going for another 2 validation numbers. if validation is worse monotonically for 3 nums ... then you try a different idea"; probe "only vitB and vitL. no vitH+"; "did you turn on gradient_checkpointing? for some layers smartly too? It helps."
- Later corrections: rejected my frozen-baseline choice ("You haven't really tested all the arms we discussed"); preferred **increasing OFT block size** / coverage over unfreezing blocks; asked where the increased solve comes in; then: "No, please add more Neumann terms or make it exact. I'm not in the mood for all this scientific (but how much better is it) we know it is better. Please do it." and "test both method and see which is faster".
- Requested a 3-arm momentum experiment to 5k with validation (20/100/500-step memory), then "continue to train the best arm"; "If experiment is inconclusive, you may extend it to 10k steps."

## Key Technical Concepts
- DINOv3 ViT-B/16: 85.7M frozen params, 12 layers, hidden 768, patch 16, 1029 tokens at 512px, RoPE, native arbitrary H×W. ViT-L/16: 303.1M, 24 layers, hidden 1024. ViT-H+ excluded by user.
- OFTv2: block-diagonal orthogonal R via Cayley of skew matrix; `theta` init 0 ⇒ R = I. **`oft_scaled=True` divides theta by `2√(block_size-1)`** (11.14 at b=32, 22.5 at b=128). fp32 master weights; rotation cast to activation dtype at the matmul.
- Cayley convention: Neumann series `I + 2Q + 2Q² + … = (I−Q)⁻¹(I+Q)`. Exact equivalent is `torch.linalg.solve(eye - q, eye + q)`.
- Head:

## Key Technical Concepts
- DINOv3 ViT-B/16: 85.7M frozen params, 12 layers, hidden 768, patch 16, 1029 tokens at 512px, RoPE, native arbitrary H×W. ViT-L/16: 303.1M, 24 layers, hidden 1024. ViT-H+ excluded by user.
- OFTv2: block-diagonal orthogonal R via Cayley of skew matrix; `theta` init 0 ⇒ R = I. **`oft_scaled=True` divides theta by `2√(block_size-1)`** (11.14 at b=32, 22.5 at b=128). fp32 master weights; rotation cast to activation dtype at the matmul.
- Cayley convention: Neumann series `I + 2Q + 2Q² + … = (I−Q)⁻¹(I+Q)`. Exact equivalent is `torch.linalg.solve(eye - q, eye + q)`.
- Head:

## Key Technical Concepts
- DINOv3 ViT-B/16: 86M backbone, 12 layers, hidden 768, patch 16, 1029 tokens @512px. ViT-L/16: 303.1M, 24 layers, hidden 1024. `hidden = self.backbone.config.hidden_size` is **derived**, not hardcoded (model.py:125).
- OFTv2: block-diagonal rotation R via Cayley; `theta` init 0 ⇒ R=I; `oft_scaled=True`; fp32 master weights. θ = 2·atan(‖Q‖). `cayley_terms=0` = exact solve (Neumann truncation diverges once ‖Q‖>1).
- Head: `LayerNorm(pool_dim) → Linear(pool_dim→12476)`; `cls_mean_max_std` = 4×hidden → 3072d (ViT-B, 38.33M) / 4096d (ViT-L, 51.12M); `head_fp32=True`, `head_dropout 0.1`.
- **Decisive capacity finding:** bar head = **35.15M** (single `L

Both answered with real numbers.

## 1. How the bar does its head — simpler than ours

`timm`'s `NormMlpClassifierHead` (35.15M total):

```
SelectAdaptivePool2d(avg)   -> global average pool to 2816-d
LayerNorm2d(2816)           -> norm on the pooled vector
Flatten -> Dropout(0.2)
Linear(2816 -> 12476)       -> 35.14M
```

So: **one global-average-pooled vector, one LayerNorm, one dropout, one Linear. No hidden layer, no projection, no multi-statistic pooling.** Our head is `LayerNorm(3072) → dropout(0.1) → Linear(3072, 12476)` = 38.33M — *bigger* than the bar's, and the extra capacity is in the 4× width (`cls_mean_max_std`), not depth.

That reframes the tower head choice: the exact bar analogue for a 5120-d tower is **mean-pool 5120 → LayerNorm → dropout → Linear(5120, 12476) = 63.9M** — architecturally identical to the winner, just wider. Option A (project down to 768 and use our multi-stat head) is *our* convention, not the bar's. I'll follow your call either way, but worth knowing.

## 2. Parameter breakdown

**Qwen tower, 460.7M:**

| module | params |
|---|---|
| `patch_embed` (Conv3d 3→1152, 2 frames × 16×16) | 1.771M |
| `pos_embed` (48×48 learned table) | 2.654M |
| `rotary_pos_emb` | ~0 |
| **`blocks`, 27 × 15.240M** | **411.467M** |
| `merger` (4608 → 5120) | 44.839M |

Per block (15.240M):

| submodule | shape | params |
|---|---|---|
| `norm1`, `norm2` | 1152 | 0.004M together |
| `attn.qkv` | 1152 → 3456 (fused) | 3.985M |
| `attn.proj` | 1152 → 1152 | 1.328M |
| `mlp.linear_fc1` | 1152 → 4304 | 4.963M |
| `mlp.linear_fc2` | 4304 → 1152 | 4.959M |

**One constraint you should see before we commit:** `4304 ÷ 32 = 134.5`, so **block-32 OFT cannot sit on the 4304-d side** of the MLP. The divisible spaces are exactly the 1152-d ones (`1152/32 = 36`) and `qkv`'s 3456-d output (`108`). So a block-32 OFT can wrap `attn.qkv`, `attn.proj`, `mlp.linear_fc1`'s input, and `mlp.linear_fc2`'s output — but not the 4304 dimension. I'll compute the exact OFT parameter count from our own `oft` module once wrapped rather than estimating it.

For reference, our ViT-B model: backbone 86.9M (frozen), head 38.33M, OFT 5.32M, unfrozen blocks making up the rest of the 12

The divisible spaces are exactly the 1152-d ones (`1152/32 = 36`) and `qkv`'s 3456-d output (`108`). So a block-32 OFT can wrap `attn.qkv`, `attn.proj`, `mlp.linear_fc1`'s input, and `mlp.linear_fc2`'s output — but not the 4304 dimension. I'll compute the exact OFT parameter count from our own `oft` module once wrapped rather than estimating it.

For reference, our ViT-B model: backbone 86.9M (frozen), head 38.33M, OFT 5.32M, unfrozen blocks making up the rest of the 12

Research the state of the art in classification HEADS and losses for large-vocabulary MULTI-LABEL image tagging, then give a ranked, evidence-grounded shortlist of what we could actually adopt. Use web_search and web_fetch (arXiv, OpenReview, HuggingFace papers, conference proceedings). Cite arXiv IDs/URLs. Distinguish measured evidence from hype.

## Our exact setup (this is what the recommendation must fit)

- Task: anime image tagging, **12,476 labels**, multi-label (each image has ~18-32 positives). Dataset: 5.32M training images, danbooru-derived, labels are noisy and heavily long-tailed.
- Backbone: **DINOv3 ViT-B/16, frozen**, with **OFTv2** adapters (orthogonal rotation, block_size 128) trained inside every Linear layer; 12 blocks optionally unfrozen. 512px input, ~1029 tokens.
- Current head: `LayerNorm(pool_dim=3072) -> Dropout(0.1) -> Linear(3072, 12476)`. The 3072 comes from concatenating CLS of the last 4 layers with mean, max, std of the patch tokens (4 x 768). Head params 38.33M, fp32.
- Loss: plain **BCE-with-logits**, no class weighting, no focal, no label smoothing, no threshold mover. Weight decay 0.
- Optimizer: SinkSGD_ADV (Muon-like, normalized update). LR head 1.2e-4, OFT 5e-4.
- **Evaluation protocol (fixed, non-negotiable): a single global threshold of 0.40**, not per-class. Metrics: macro F1/MCC/P/R and micro F1/MCC/P/R. Macro is the dominated axis.
- Our numbers at 0.40 vs a strong 692.6M ConvNeXtV2-Huge baseline on the same 50k images: macro F1 0.2815 vs 0.5579, micro F1 0.6133 vs 0.6886. We LEAD the baseline on micro PRECISION (0.7068 vs 0.7033) but our **macro recall is 0.2266 vs its 0.5333**. So the failure mode is unmistakable: **we under-predict rare/tail labels**. The baseline head is only `avgpool -> LayerNorm -> Dropout(0.2) -> Linear(2816,12476)`, i.e. architecturally simpler than ours, and it wins on macro anyway — suggesting the head is not where its advantage comes from.
- Training budget: a full pass is ~9h; probes are 5k-10k steps. Anything we adopt must be cheap enough to test in a few hours and must not need a second backbone.
- We already have SigLIP2 and MetaCLIP-2 image-text models on disk (I:\Models), in case label

Paused branch is recorded at `docs/paused-qwen-tower.md` (status, what's measured, the three open decisions, exact resume steps) and the sweep is running — h1 started 00:25.

Meanwhile the CPU diagnostic finished, and it's the most decision-relevant result we've had today:

## Where the macro gap actually lives

Per-tag F1 on the 50k cache at 0.40, binned by training frequency (ours 0.2820 vs bar 0.5589 overall — matches our known numbers, so the readout is sound):

| train freq | tags | ours F1 | bar F1 | gap | ours recall | bar recall |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 (unseen) | 1,679 | 0.2409 | 0.5630 | **0.3221** | 0.1918 | 0.5396 |
| 10–99 | 7,060 | 0.2419 | 0.5613 | **0.3193** | 0.1891 | 0.5340 |
| 100–999 | 2,980 | 0.3458 | 0.5360 | 0.1902 | 0.2812 | 0.5126 |
| 1k–9.9k | 646 | 0.4755 | 0.6015 | 0.1260 | 0.4195 | 0.5927 |
| ≥10k | 89 | 0.6987 | 0.7538 | 0.0552 | 0.6950 | 0.7686 |

**Share of the total macro gap: 65.4% from the 10–99 bin alone, 15.7% from unseen tags, 16.4% from 100–999. About 97% comes from tags with under 1,000 training examples.** The gap shrinks monotonically as frequency rises — on our 89 most common tags we're within 0.055 of the bar, i.e. nearly matched.

Also note our recall on rare tags is 0.19 while our *precision* leads the bar on micro (0.7068 vs 0.7033). We aren't wrong about what we fire — we just don't fire.

**What this settles:** the deficit is unambiguously tail-concentrated, so it is not a uniform representation shortfall that a wider backbone would fix evenly — and combined with the research finding that our per-tag-oracle threshold ceiling is only 0.3879, no amount of re-scoring or threshold work reaches the bar's 0.5579. The missing 0.17 is in the scores for rare classes.

**And to your question — yes, there are many ways to do a head**, and it helps to separate them: *pooling* (cls/mean/max/std), *depth* (linear vs MLP), *decoding* (ML-Decoder-style query attention), *normalisation* (cosine classifier), *initialisation* (label-text embeddings from SigLIP2), and *loss-side rebalancing* (CB-NTR, ASL). The evidence only strongly supports the last one for **tail** recall — which is precisely our failure mode. ML-Decoder

## Primary Request and Intent
- Train an anime tagger: frozen/modified **DINOv3** backbone + **OFTv2** adapter + **SinkSGD_ADV** optimizer ("muon-like"), BF16, maximum efficiency.
- **Goal (AGENTS.md):** "beat convnextv2_huge on their Macro@0.40 (F1/MCC/P/R), Micro@0.40 against our model at treshholds@0.40, with validation test".
- Fixed threshold **0.40 only**, no per-tag tuning ("for speed, we need to compare our micro and macro to their Macro@0.40, Micro@0.40. I cant sit around all day and be testing treshholds.").
- **New pivot (turn 144+):** head back from Qwen to ViT-B; use the old 10k-era arms as a fast benchmark to test new heads. "Remember all the tests we ran on the small 10k dataset? We can use that as a benchmark to test our new heads".
- **Overnight authorization:** "Continue testing head variants. I am going to sleep. Be exhaustive in your search." Budget: "All night — 8-10 h". Scope: "Try everything. You have all night- what's there to be afraid of?"
- **Extensions:** "Opt 2" = extend only inconclusive arms to 10k. Then: "After you're done with the heads, pick the best head and try training last N {1,2,3,...} layers of vit b + OFT".
- **Reporting:** wants a centralized hand-written place for all arm-test results ("We could try 10k-report.md or something like that"), prefers "simpler methods over complicated machinery"; REPORT.md is generated so leave the generator mostly alone.
- Token-audit scope: **"Reports only"**.
- Paused Qwen branch must be recorded as a docs file (done: `docs/paused-qwen-tower.md`).
- Standing: "when your context is near full, you should be wary, and be opting to recording things."

## Key Technical Concepts
- DINOv3 ViT-B/16: 86–91M backbone, 12 layers, hidden 768, patch 16, 1029 tokens @512px. ViT-L/16: 303M.
- OFTv2: rotates **`in_features`** dimension; `block_size` auto-adjusted by `_adjust_block_size` (mirrors OneTrainer); OFT params = `d*(b-1)/2`; `cayley_terms=0` = exact solve; fp32 master weights.
- Head: `LayerNorm(pool_dim) → [Linear(pool_dim,head_hidden) → GELU → Dropout] → Linear(·, 12476)`, `pool_dim = 768 × len(parts)`, `POOL_PARTS = ("cls","mean","max","std")`.
- Our head `cls_mean_max_std` = 3072 → **38.33M*

## What I built

**A 30-minute recurring prompt** — `Check arm sweep and pick next action`. Rather than a bare "look at the logs", the prompt carries the actual agenda so a check-in doesn't have to re-derive the plan:

1. Run **one** command (`checkin.py`) to get everything.
2. Verify before trusting — step counts must reach the cap; 50k numbers must come from `validation.json`, never scraped console text.
3. Score anything finished that isn't scored, including the `best_val` (macro-peak) reading.
4. Relaunch the queue if it died, but never while an arm is mid-training.
5. Once all arms are scored: build the table, compare against **h6's seed-noise band**, then start the unfreeze sweep.
6. If the GPU is busy and nothing's due → **CPU-only work**: frequency-bin gap, per-tag precision/recall, label co-occurrence among failing tags, false-negative inspection.
7. Theorize and **write down the next arm before running it**.

**`scratch/sophie/checkin.py`** — one read-only command that prints GPU/disk, every arm's steps vs total, its 1k macro@0.40 peak **and** how far it has fallen from that peak, 50k scores, the paused run's integrity, and a "due next" verdict. So a check-in costs one tool call instead of six.

```
GPU 21331/24564 MiB, util 84%          disk C: free 405.2 GB
h1_control_cls_mean_max_std  3320/5200   1k peak 0.0298   running
                             peak at step 1040, last 0.0227 (24% below peak)
paused long run 4a13a120: .PAUSE=True, 10 ckpt(s), latest step 086241 (must not change)
--- due next ---  training in progress: h1 — nothing to launch
```

## One thing I fixed while doing this

There was an older 6-hour reminder still carrying claims that are now **wrong**: "nothing is running by design", "the entire gap is backbone capacity", and "configs/probes awaiting go-ahead". In six hours that would have contradicted the live sweep and possibly sent me down a dead path. I rewrote it as a narrow backstop that only verifies `runs/4a13a120` is still paused at 86,241, explicitly marks the old "heads are pointless" verdict as superseded, and points at ARM-RESULTS.md as current truth.

## Key Technical Concepts
- DINOv3 ViT-B/16: 86–91M backbone, 12 layers, hidden 768, patch 16, 1029 tokens @512px.
- OFTv2 rotates `in_features`; `block_size` auto-adjusted; `cayley_terms=0` = exact solve; fp32 master weights.
- Head: `LayerNorm(pool_dim) → [Linear→GELU→Dropout] → Linear(·,12476)`; `pool_dim = 768 × len(parts)`; `POOL_PARTS=("cls","mean","max","std")`.
- Control head `cls_mean_max_std` = 3072 → **38.34M**; bar head = **35.15M** (avgpool→LayerNorm2d(2816)→Dropout(0.2)→Linear(2816,12476)

==========================================================================================
session=session-2645804d-bf0d-4aaf-a26c-dcfe10504be6 line=11815 role=assistant time=1792674128133 chars=2065
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
**The bar's head is almost aggressively plain.** Measured from the actual timm module:

```
SelectAdaptivePool2d(avg)  ->  one 2816-d vector
LayerNorm2d(2816)
Flatten
Dropout(0.2)
Linear(2816 -> 12476)      ->  35.14M
```

`NormMlpClassifierHead` with `pre_logits = Identity()`. **No hidden layer. No CLS token. No max, no std, no multi-statistic pooling.** 35.15M params on a 657M backbone (692.6M total).

Ours was `LayerNorm(3072) → Dropout(0.1) → Linear(3072, 12476)` = **38.33M**, fed by CLS + mean + max + std pooling. *Bigger and more sophisticated than the thing beating us.* Which is exactly why I've been sceptical of head work all night — and why h3 (`cls` only, 768-d, **9.6M**) coming out on top is the least surprising result in the table.

**What the head literature actually offers** (from `docs/REPORTS/heads-*.md`):

| family | idea | verdict |
|---|---|---|
| **ML-Decoder** (Ridnik et al.) | 100–400 queries cross-attend the 1,029 patch tokens; per-label logit from a *label-conditioned* feature instead of one global vector | best-evidenced architecture change, but +0.5–1.0 mAP at 9,600 labels and ~+0.01 macro for us |
| **Single linear head** ablation | 86.0 → 86.7/86.8 mAP at 9,600 labels — the linear head is already near-optimal | says don't bother |
| **DECAF / CLIP-Decoder** | initialise Linear rows from image→text projections, or add a low-rank label-feature term | pays for *unseen/few-shot* labels; we're fully supervised |
| **MoE heads, per-label adapters** | more parameters per label | rejected — capacity is demonstrably not our bottleneck |
| **Any-label / zero-shot heads** | label-text + linear | only a tie-breaker for the ~1–2k tags with a handful of positives |

Plus two non-architectural ones that ranked above every head: **CB-NTR** (BALMS/DBL) and **post-hoc logit adjustment** (Menon et al.).

The paper-level conclusion, and now the measured one: **2024–2026 gains in this task family came from backbones and data, not heads.** Our own arms said the same thing in nine hours — heads spanned ±5%, ASL spanned +51%.

## Key Technical Concepts
- DINOv3 ViT-B/16 (~87M backbone, 12 layers, 768 hidden, 16 patch, 1029 tokens @512px, 12476 labels).
- OFTv2 rotates `in_features`; `block_size=128`; `cayley_terms=0` = exact solve.
- Head: `LayerNorm(pool_dim) → Linear(·,12476)`; `pool_dim = 768 × len(parts)`; `POOL_PARTS=("cls","mean","max","std")` = 3072 → 38.33M params; `cls` only = 768 → 9.58M.
- ASL (arXiv 2009.14119): `xs_neg=(1-p+clip).clamp(max=1)`, one-sided gamma, `detach_focal=True`. **Effective gamma is `gamma_neg`**, `gamma_pos=0.0`, `clip=0.05`.

| group | params | lr | what |
|---|---:|---|---|
| 0 | 0.049M | 4.6e-4 | OFT rotations, one tensor |
| 1 | 85.06M | 2.8e-5 | last 12 transformer blocks, 204 tensors |
| 2 | 9.60M | 1.1e-4 | `LayerNorm(768)` plus `Linear(768, 12476)` |

Total live: 94.7M parameters.

The OFTv2 adapters are, in this arm, nearly asleep. There are 73 rotation modules and 5.32M params of them in the file, but only 49k receive a gradient: the patch-embed convolution's rotation. The reason is deliberate — `unfreeze_last_blocks` freezes every `oft.weight` inside a block it unfreezes, because a rotation sitting beside an already-trainable weight is a redundant parameterisation. With `unfreeze_blocks: 12` and a 12-block ViT-B, that leaves no rotation live anywhere except the patch embed.

So do not read arm 01 as an OFT experiment. It is a direct fine-tune of all 12 blocks plus a linear head. OFT earns its keep only in the u0/u4/u6 arms, where the frozen blocks must be adapted by rotation alone — which is exactly what arms 05-07 are for.

## How it works today

One tower, one head. `wdtag/model.py` loads DINOv3, wraps every `nn.Linear` in the backbone with `OFTLinear`, unfreezes the last N blocks, pools the token grid into a vector (768 for `cls`, 3072 for `cls_mean_max_std`), LayerNorms it, and puts one `Linear(pool, 12476)` on top. ASL sits on those logits. Three optimizer groups, one checkpoint, one validation path.

An ensemble adds a second branch to that picture and touches nothing else:

```
uint8 batch [B,3,512,512]
   |                       |
DINOv3 normalization   SigLIP normalization (mean/std 0.5, range -1..1)
   |                       |
12 blocks + OFT        24 blocks + OFT (optional)
   |                       |
pool -> 768 or 3072    MAP head -> 1024
   \                      /
      concat 1792 or 4096
             |
   Linear(concat, 12476) -> ASL
```

Concretely that is a second backbone in `DinoTagger`, `apply_oft_to_backbone` called twice (it is already generic), `adapter_state_dict` keyed by tower, five optimizer groups instead of three, and a head input of 1792 with cls pooling or 4096 with CMSS, so 22.4M or 51.1M.

## Your choice is a good one for a reason that is not the benchmark number

L/16 at 512 is 882,313,218 parameters total, of which the vision tower is about 303M: 24 blocks of width 1024, 1024 tokens at 512px. It is the strongest L variant in the family (INet 83.5 against 83.1 at 384), and more importantly it takes exactly the tensor our cache already holds. Same 512 squash, same token count as DINOv3, no second resize path, and both towers see identical geometry — which matters, because the geometry audit says our squash costs real accuracy and I would rather both towers pay it identically than have one see 384-cropped and one see 512-squashed.

Here is the part I want you to see before we commit, because we just measured it on the DINO side.

OFT inside frozen blocks is not a forward-only proposition. A rotation in block 3 needs a gradient, so the backward pass still walks blocks 4 to 24 to deliver it, and every activation is retained for that walk. The evidence is arm u1, running now: dropping 11 unfrozen blocks

## Key Technical Concepts
- DINOv3 ViT-B/16 = 85660416 params, 211 tensors: 12 identical blocks × 7088640, plus 596736 outside (patch embed 590592+768, cls_token 768, mask_token 768, 4 register_tokens 3072, final norm 1536).
- Per block 7088640: separate q/k/v/o each 589824 (k has no bias, q/v/o biases 2304) = 2361600 attention; mlp up 768→3072 = 2362368, down 3072→768 = 2360064 = 4722432; norm1+norm2 1536 each; layer_scale1/layer_scale2 768 each.
- OFT: `block_size 128`, 8128 params per 128-row block → 768-in module 48768, 3072-in (mlp.down) 195072; per block 438912; 73 modules total 5315712; patch-embed conv rotation 48768.
- `unfreeze_last_blocks` freezes `oft.weight` inside every block it unfreezes, so

session=session-2645804d-bf0d-4aaf-a26c-dcfe10504be6 line=15938 role=assistant time=1792693927927 chars=4271

One correction first, because it matters: with both towers frozen and no adapter inside either, both are cacheable. The blocker was never the tower, it is the adapter. Put OFT inside DINOv3 and DINOv3 becomes uncacheable while SigLIP2, still clean, stays cacheable. That is the "one but not the other" case, and it comes from OFT, not from the tower.

So yes, the picture you describe is right: one forward pass per image, one pooled vector per tower, 768 from DINOv3's cls and 768 from SigLIP2's MAP head, concatenated to 1536 floats, and training becomes a function on that vector. 5.32M images of that is 16.3 GB on disk, fp16.

## But rotating the embedding does not do the same thing as OFT

It is cacheable, yes, and it is also nearly pointless, for a reason worth seeing once.

A rotation applied after pooling is just a linear map on the 768-vector, and a linear map followed by a linear classifier is a linear classifier. The head learns W, the rotation applies R, and W times R is some other matrix the head could have learned directly, with no orthogonal constraint and no extra parameters. So a post-pool rotation can only help if something nonlinear sits between it and the classifier. Put a bottleneck there and it becomes a genuine low-rank adapter on the features, which is a fine idea, but it is a head-architecture idea and it is not OFT.

OFT inside the tower is a different function entirely, because the rotation is applied to every token at every layer, before attention. The queries and keys are rotated, so the attention pattern itself changes; the MLP inputs change; the residual stream carries a different signal into the next block. That is a change of representation, and it cannot be reconstructed from the pooled output, because the pooled output is the summary after all of that has already happened. One vector cannot carry the token grid.

There is a variant that sounds like it should work and does not: caching the token grid at the input to the last block, so the last block stays trainable. The grid for one image at 1024 tokens by 768 dims in fp16 is 1.5 MB, which is 8 TB for the corpus. That is why nobody does intermediate caching.

session=session-35636ebe-eae5-4a06-9d89-6974c58541ac line=9552 role=assistant time=1792929600259 chars=3121

Then everything changes, and for the better — cached feature training is the highest-leverage move available, and it makes the experiments I have been rationing become free.

What caching buys, concretely. A forward-only pass over 5321713 images at 512 costs about four hours once. Pooled features at cls plus mean plus max plus std are 3072 numbers per image, so 32.6 GB in fp16, or 8.2 GB if we cache only the 768-dimensional pooled vector. I: has 332 GB free. After that, a training epoch over the whole corpus is minutes of GPU because there is no backbone backward and no image decoding at all, and a 50k validation becomes seconds.

That changes which questions are worth asking. Right now a single head experiment costs fourteen hours; cached it costs minutes, so the things I ruled out for cost become the obvious next moves.

The head, first. A linear projection over pooled features cannot represent a relation between two people, and relational tags are where the bar beats us two to one while we over-fire on the component concepts. A two-layer MLP with dropout costs a few million parameters and can be tested in an afternoon rather than a night. That is the cheapest direct attack on the composition gap we have.

Calibration second, baked into training. Per-tag rate matching measured 0.3781 held-out against 0.3465, and cached training means we can also afford a per-tag bias table fitted on the training split, which is the honest version of what I measured on validation.

Ensembling third, which becomes trivial with caches. SigLIP2 features from the finished production checkpoint and DinoV3 features from the same images can be concatenated, and a head trained over both is the gogeta idea done without touching either backbone. A single cached pass over DinoV3 plus a concatenated head is hours, not days, and it is the one path that could plausibly reach the bar's macro because it adds genuinely independent evidence rather than more capacity on the same features.

One important sequencing point: cache from the finished production checkpoint, not from 14301, because the features are the product of everything the run learns, and the last five thousand steps still move macr

==========================================================================================
session=session-35636ebe-eae5-4a06-9d89-6974c58541ac line=10592 role=assistant time=1792940697280 chars=3605
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Discussion it is, and you have the ladder right. Let me put the numbers on it, since the choice is really just which rung buys which question.

Rung one, no cache, is what we have been doing. Every experiment pays the backbone, 90 to 120 img/s for a full training step, and one experiment is a night.

Rung two is the pooled embedding, one vector per image. For SigLIP2 that is 768 floats, 1536 bytes in fp16, 8.2 GB for all 5.3 million images, and one forward pass at 270 img/s costs about 5.5 hours. This is what I built and tested today.

Rung three is all four poolings rather than just the class token: the class token, the mean over patches, the max over patches and the std over patches, concatenated. That is 3072 floats, 6144 bytes, 32.7 GB, and it costs exactly the same forward pass as rung two because the pooling happens after the trunk. The production model used cls only. The max pool is the interesting one, because max asks whether a concept appears anywhere in the image, which is exactly the question a tag decision asks.

Rung four is the patch tokens themselves, 1024 tokens of 768 dimensions, 1.57 MB per image, which is 8.4 TB for the corpus and therefore not a corpus at all. It is only viable for a subset: 25000 images is 39 GB, which we can afford. This is the only rung that lets you train an attention pool or a small transformer that decides where to look, rather than how to weight a vector that already averaged the image.

Now the part that matters for your SigLIP2 instinct. The head trained on a cache cannot push gradients into the backbone, so rung two and three buy us the head, the loss, the calibration, the class weighting and the tower ensemble, and nothing else. But we should cache from the trained checkpoint rather than from the raw model, because the 16000 checkpoint's features already contain the OFT adaptation that scored 0.3840. Caching raw SigLIP2 would test a weaker representation and tell us less.

Training on a cache is `wdtag.train_cached`: it reads the memmap, applies a LayerNorm, then a linear projection or one hidden layer, with ASL and a cosine schedule, and scores the 50k validation cache at 0.40 every epoch and prints the predicted
